An irreversible intervention converts uncertainty into permanent exposure. English commonly says “we can change course later” without marking the time window, recovery cost, or whether some losses cannot be restored.

A reversibility grade should distinguish:

TermExplanation
immediately reversibleimmediately reversible at low cost
reversiblereversible within a finite window
technically reversibletechnically reversible but socially or economically locked in
partially compensablepartially compensable but not restorable
irreversibleirreversible under known methods
reversibility unknownAvailable evidence does not establish whether restoration, compensation, or rollback is possible.

The distinction interacts with uncertainty. A low-confidence decision may be rational when reversible and reckless when irreversible. A high expected value can be unacceptable if downside crosses a catastrophic threshold. Risk language that omits reversibility is incomplete.

Path dependence and lock-in. A system is path-dependent when its present state depends on the sequence through which it arrived, not only on current conditions. Lock-in is a stronger practical state in which alternatives may be better but switching costs, network effects, standards, contracts, skills, or political coalitions stabilise the incumbent arrangement.

English has the terms, but they remain specialist vocabulary. Everyday causal narratives favour current reasons: “we use the system because it is efficient.” Path dependence asks whether it appears efficient because infrastructure, training, compatibility, and expectations accumulated around it. The present can become evidence for a choice that the past manufactured.

A path marker would distinguish:

TermExplanation
selected-nowThe current configuration was selected through a present choice.
inherited-pathThe configuration is inherited from earlier decisions and accumulated dependencies.
locked-by-costTransition is constrained by economic or operational switching cost.
locked-by-networkTransition is constrained by compatibility, coordination, or network effects.
locked-by-lawTransition is constrained by legal or regulatory commitments.
and locked-by-identityExplicitly records the state and locked-by-identity.

The final form captures cases where communities resist change because the incumbent practice has become part of who they believe themselves to be. Such lock-in cannot be solved by proving technical inferiority.

Externalities and displaced grammar. An externality occurs when costs or benefits fall outside the decision-maker’s accounting boundary. Ordinary syntax mirrors the boundary. “The factory produced affordable goods” is grammatically complete even when pollution, health costs, and ecosystem damage appear in other sentences with other subjects. The language separates what the system couples.

An externality vector should mark direction: This makes the distribution of agency and burden part of the proposition rather than an optional moral

TermExplanation
Benefit recipientThe benefit accrues to the decision-maker.
Non-consenting costThe cost is borne by neighbouring parties who did not consent.
Future-person costThe cost is transferred to people who are not yet able to participate.
Publicly borne riskA public institution bears or guarantees the downside risk.
Knowledge asymmetryRelevant knowledge is distributed asymmetrically among affected parties.

appendix. It also prevents aggregate welfare from hiding concentrated harm.

From blame to intervention. A richer causal grammar could become oppressive if used primarily to assign blame. Its better purpose is intervention. Different causal layers imply different remedies. Remove the trigger, redesign the mechanism, change the incentive, add a safeguard, alter the institutional memory, or redistribute control. A single causal noun encourages a single culprit; a causal map supports layered prevention.

The empirical question is whether such markers improve reasoning. Participants could analyse accidents, algorithmic harms, public-health failures, and environmental events. Researchers could measure whether causal-layer language increases identification of structural interventions without erasing individual responsibility. Legal and organisational teams could compare ordinary incident reports with tagged reports for actionability and disagreement detection.

Language should not decide guilt. It should make the architecture of action harder to falsify by omission.

Relations, Systems, and Candidate Conceptual Operators

A noun gives the impression that the world has already decided where one thing ends and another begins. Tree, company, disease, market, intelligence, community: each word packages a region of reality as a unit available for counting, predication, and possession. This capacity is a triumph of cognition. It is also a source of systematic error when relations and processes are treated as intrinsic properties of isolated things.

A person

“has” a disease, a company “owns” data, a country “possesses” resources, a model “contains” bias, a community “shares” values.

The grammar offers owner and owned, subject and property. Yet many of these phenomena are relational. Disease can depend on organism, environment, classification, and threshold. Data rights can involve subjects, collectors, custodians, processors, beneficiaries, and affected non-subjects. Bias can arise through interaction among objective, dataset, model, interface, deployment, and social feedback. Values can be contested practices rather than items stored in a collective mind.

Possession, constitution, and stewardship. Languages with possessive classes show that possession is not universally treated as one relation. Body parts and kin may receive different marking from transferable objects; food and drink may be classified by intended use or control; spatial and social relations may have dedicated constructions. The common label

“alienable versus inalienable” captures only part of this diversity (Nichols and Bickel 2013).

Contemporary English needs at least these relations:

TermExplanation
titlelegally recognised ownership
controlpractical ability to use, exclude, or alter
custodytemporary responsibility for keeping
stewardshipauthority constrained by duties to others or the future
authorshiporigination of an expression or design
provenancecausal contribution to an artefact
membershipbelonging within a collective
constitutionbeing a part without which the whole is not the same kind of thing
dependencyrequiring a relation for continued existence or function
exposurebearing effects without ownership or control

“The company’s data” can then be unpacked.

The company may hold custody and processing control; individuals may be the data subjects; a public institution may impose stewardship duties; several actors may contribute provenance; communities may bear exposure. Possessive apostrophe should not settle the politics.

Part, member, ingredient, and participant. English part of similarly combines different mereological relations. A wheel is a component of a bicycle. A person is a member of a committee. Water is an ingredient in soup. A scene is a segment of a film. A cell participates in a tissue. A subcontractor participates in a supply chain. A rule belongs to a legal system. These relations support different inferences.

If a component is removed, the machine may fail. If a member leaves, the organisation may persist. An ingredient loses some independent identity in a mixture. A participant may influence a process without being contained by it. A boundary dispute becomes confused when one form of parthood is substituted for another.

Collective identity requires especially careful distinctions between member, role-holder, stakeholder, affected party, beneficiary, and represented party. Someone can be affected without membership, represented without authorisation, and formally included without power. An institutional grammar that records only members will systematically miss its moral perimeter.

Boundaries are operations. The natural world contains gradients, overlaps, nested systems, and phase transitions. Human institutions need discrete decisions. We draw a line around a wetland, a disease category, a labour market, a species, or a conflict. The line can be scientifically useful and legally necessary even when no equally sharp boundary exists in the phenomenon.

The mistake is not drawing boundaries. It is forgetting the operation that drew them. A category should therefore carry a boundary mode: natural-discontinuity, measurement-threshold, legal-rule, operational-convenience, statistical-cluster, self-identification, external-attribution, or contested.

This marker changes debate. Two researchers may agree on all observations but use different thresholds. Two communities may contest whether identity is self-ascribed or institutionally assigned. A regulator may intentionally create a legal boundary that does not claim to mirror a natural kind. Without a boundary mode, disagreements are misdiagnosed as factual when they are methodological or normative.

Emergence as a system-level relation. A property is emergent when it belongs to an organised system in a way not straightforwardly attributable to isolated parts. Temperature, flocking, traffic waves, institutional reputation, and market price are standard examples at different levels. The term is often abused as a synonym for surprising or unexplained.

A useful emergence marker should specify the relation:

TermExplanation
macro-pattern generatedmacro-pattern generated by local interaction
property predictableproperty predictable only at a higher-level model
property robustproperty robust under replacement of parts
property exerting constraints on partsproperty exerting constraints on parts through organised structure
property merely redescribedproperty merely redescribed at aggregate level

This prevents

“emergence” from functioning as a mystical stop sign.

It also prevents reductionism from assuming that because a macro-property depends on parts, talk of the whole is dispensable. A traffic jam is made of cars but is not located inside any one car. A company policy is enacted by people and documents but cannot be assigned to one molecule or employee.

Scale-indexed truth. Many apparently contradictory claims are true at different scales. Individual ants follow local rules; a colony allocates labour. A neuron fires; a person decides. A firm cuts costs; an industry becomes fragile. A local intervention lowers risk; a network adapts and restores the original pattern. Without a scale index, explanations slide between levels.

A distinction among micro, meso, and macro scale is only a beginning. Scale can be spatial, temporal, organisational, informational, or causal. A claim should identify the unit whose behaviour is described and the aggregation rule connecting levels.

“Competition improves efficiency” may hold for a transaction while failing for infrastructure resilience. “The model is accurate” may hold on average while failing for a vulnerable subgroup. “The country is richer” may coexist with most citizens losing.

Scale-indexed language resists the fallacy of composition and the fallacy of division. Properties of parts need not belong to wholes; properties of wholes need not belong to every part.

Feedback and the disappearance of the first cause. Linear grammar prefers A caused B. Systems contain loops: A changes B, B changes C, and C changes A. Positive feedback amplifies deviations; negative feedback counteracts them; delayed feedback can create oscillation; adaptive agents can alter the rules in response to measurement.

Once a loop is established, asking for

“the cause” may be less useful than asking what maintains the cycle and where intervention has leverage. A feedback marker would identify reinforcing, balancing, delayed, and adaptive loops. It could also state whether an observed relation is one pass through a loop or a stable regime.

This matters for polarisation, poverty traps, ecological collapse, addiction, platform engagement, and arms races. A sentence such as

“people consume sensational content because they prefer it” ignores the loop through recommendation, production incentives, habituation, and social visibility. Preference is both input and product.

Thresholds and phase changes. Linear language also encourages proportional expectations: a little more cause yields a little more effect. Many systems contain thresholds beyond which behaviour changes qualitatively. An ecosystem collapses, a material changes phase, a network cascades, an institution loses legitimacy, or a bank run becomes self-fulfilling.

A threshold-distance marker would state whether a system is far from a known threshold, approaching one, within an uncertainty band, or already beyond it. Importantly, the threshold itself may be unknown or moving.

“Risk is currently low” can be dangerously misleading if the system is close to a tipping point with uncertain location.

The marker should distinguish probability of crossing from severity after crossing. Low-probability irreversible thresholds require different reasoning from frequent reversible fluctuations.

Commons and the grammar of no one. Resources such as atmosphere, biodiversity, shared knowledge, public trust, and open infrastructure do not fit ordinary possession. If nobody owns them, grammar can imply that nobody is responsible. If humanity

“owns” them, the possessive remains too vague to allocate duties.

The concept of commons is already available but undergrammaticalised. A commons relation should mark access rights, contribution duties, depletion effects, governance, exclusion, and future claims.

“We used public data” should not be complete without asking who maintained it, who consented, who can withdraw, and who receives benefit.

A commons-burden marker could attach costs to users who would otherwise appear as free beneficiaries. Open-source software offers a simple example: millions may depend on a library maintained by a handful of people. The code is accessible, but maintenance capacity is rival and exhaustible.

“Free” describes price, not burden.

Unknown, unknowable, and not yet conceptualised. The final relation hidden by nouns is the relation between a question and its possible answer. We speak of

“the unknown” as though it were a region containing facts waiting to be collected.

Some unknowns are unanswered questions with known methods. Some require new instruments. Some are underdetermined. Some are inaccessible for physical or computational reasons. Some are not yet questions because we lack the concepts to formulate them.

The phrase unknown unknowns gestures toward the last category but remains paradoxical. Once named, a specific unknown unknown becomes at least a known gap. What matters is the epistemic perimeter: evidence that our present ontology, variable set, or question space is incomplete. An anomaly, repeated model failure, category instability, or perspective excluded from the inquiry can signal such a perimeter.

A language for deeper thinking should therefore contain not only unknown but method-known, instrument-missing, underdetermined, computationally-intractable, concept-missing, and question-contested. The distinctions prevent ignorance from being mistaken for mystery—and mystery from being prematurely converted into a routine data gap.

Nouns let us carry the world. Relations remind us that what we carry was cut from a larger fabric. The final

section of this chapter turns these insights into a compact inventory of missing variables.

The following proposals are not claims that English speakers lack the corresponding ideas. Most can be expressed through technical prose. The problem is that expression is expensive, inconsistent, or confined to specialist disciplines. Each proposal asks whether a compact lexical item, operator, or grammatical prompt could make a distinction easier to retain across reasoning.

The inventory also avoids a common trap. A new word is not automatically a new thought. Coinages become useful only when they organise contrasts, support inference, and survive use. The strongest candidates therefore have four properties: a recurring cognitive failure, partial analogues in natural languages or formal systems, a compact compositional design, and an experimentally testable benefit.

Source mode. A claim should be able to carry a compact answer to

“How was this known?”

Natural evidentiality supplies the model: direct perception, non-visual sensation, inference, report, and quotation can be grammatical categories. Scientific and computational settings require a wider inventory: observed, instrument-measured, experimentally manipulated, statistically inferred, simulated, retrieved, reported, remembered, and generated.

A future interface could make source mode visible as a compact annotation or a spoken marker in specialised settings. A mortality claim reported from a trial is epistemically different from one produced by causal inference. Making the distinction visible does not validate either claim; it preserves its epistemic species.

The cognitive hypothesis is that repeated source marking improves source memory and reduces the tendency to treat fluent repetition as independent evidence. Experiments should test delayed recall, transmission across summarisation chains, and resistance to authority effects. Existing work on Turkish evidentiality gives a plausible starting point but not a settled answer.

Provenance depth and independence. Source mode identifies a channel; provenance depth identifies a lineage. A newspaper may be one step from a press release, three steps from primary data, and zero steps from an anonymous social post. Five visible documents may have one evidential root. Ordinary citation formats represent links but rarely express independence at a glance.

A claim could record that it is two transmission steps from one primary root and has undergone both summarisation and translation. The most important distinction is between multiplicity and independence.

“Confirmed by five sources” should not be licensed when all five copy one origin.

The marker would change research review, intelligence analysis, and AI retrieval. Its hypothesis is that visible source depth reduces false corroboration and better preserves uncertainty through chains of abstraction. A cryptographic or database layer can verify parts of the metadata, but the linguistic category remains useful even when the record is incomplete: depth:unknown is more honest than silence.

Confidence state and uncertainty kind. A bare probability answers too little. Uncertainty may arise from sampling, measurement, model misspecification, missing variables, disagreement among experts, stochastic processes, or unknown mechanisms. Two claims with confidence 0.7 may require different actions.

The proposed state pairs an ordinal or numeric confidence with an uncertainty type:

TermExplanation
Sampling uncertaintyThe confidence estimate is conditioned mainly by sampling variation.
Model uncertaintyThe numeric confidence is similar, but uncertainty concerns the structure of the model.
Deep uncertaintyRelevant alternatives, models, or probabilities are not sufficiently characterised.

Deep uncertainty applies when actors do not agree on the model, probabilities, or relevant outcome set. It should not be disguised as a wide error bar inside one accepted model.

The hypothesis is that separating confidence from uncertainty type improves decision choice. Participants should prefer information gathering under measurement uncertainty, robustness under model uncertainty, and reversible experiments under deep uncertainty. Ordinary

“not sure” encourages one generic response to heterogeneous ignorance.

Revision state. Knowledge is not only more or less certain; it occupies a position in a revision process. A claim can be an exploratory lead, a working assumption, a preregistered prediction, a provisional conclusion, an operational decision, a contested result, a superseded model, or a retracted assertion.

Scientific documents encode some revision states through genre and publication history, but sentences detach from their lifecycle. A preliminary estimate quoted years later may look final. Revision status such as exploratory or superseded should therefore travel with the claim.

The expected effect is improved temporal calibration of knowledge. It may also reduce the rhetorical exploitation of science’s self-correction. A superseded claim is not simply

“false information scientists once believed”; it is a state transition in an error-correcting practice.

Conversely, publication is not an automatic transition to settled truth.

Absence status.

“No X” can mean an empty set, a missing expected item, non-detection, nonexistence, deactivation, or inapplicability. Medical tests, security scans, databases, and scientific null results are routinely misinterpreted because these states share negative syntax.

A compact family could be:

zero-XA defined collection contains no members.
missing-XThe item was expected in the relevant context but is absent.
undetected-XA search found no item within a declared sensitivity or coverage.
uninstantiated-XThe type is admissible, but no current token is known.
Model-relative non-existenceModel M contains no entity of the specified kind; the statement does not establish metaphysical non-existence.
inapplicable-XThe predicate is not applicable to the object.

The hypothesis is straightforward: explicit absence status should reduce false reassurance from negative tests, confusion between missing and zero data, and ontological inflation from treating every null field as an unknown object.

Knowability and modal mode. Unknown says nothing about whether an answer can be obtained. Possible says nothing about whether possibility is logical, physical, technological, institutional, or merely not yet excluded. A combined modal–epistemic operator should distinguish:

TermExplanation
unknown withunknown with known method
unknown because anunknown because an instrument is missing
underdetermined byunderdetermined by all available evidence
computationally intractablecomputationally intractable under current bounds
unknowableunknowable under a stated physical or logical assumption

Concept missing: the current vocabulary does not formulate the relevant variable.

TermExplanation
counterfactualcounterfactual under model M
impossibleimpossible under model M but open under rival models

The crucial distinction is model relativity.

“Impossible under model M” is weaker and often more accurate than an unqualified “impossible.”

A perpetual-motion machine is impossible under accepted thermodynamics; a policy is often called impossible only under current institutions.

The hypothesis is that model-indexed modality reduces premature closure. It should help teams distinguish

“we lack data” from “we may be asking the wrong question,” and distinguish robust physical constraints from social arrangements presented as necessities.

Matter-same. Matter-same records continuity of substrate. It applies naturally to artefacts whose identity depends partly on retained material: a restored painting, a biological sample, a historical building. The marker must include tolerance because no macroscopic object preserves every particle.

Material continuity may be judged from the retained mass and the preservation of critical original components; one example retains seventy-two percent of its mass and all designated critical components.

This operator prevents material continuity from being confused with function or role. A ship rebuilt plank by plank may remain role-same and flow-same while losing matter-sameness. In conservation, heritage, and forensic evidence, that difference is decisive.

The experimental prediction is that an explicit matter operator will reduce inconsistent identity judgments driven by superficial wording. It may also reveal genuine value pluralism: people can agree on the facts while weighting continuities differently.

Form-same. Form-same marks preserved structure, organisation, or relational pattern. A copied key, reconstructed molecule, replicated database schema, and reorganised team can be form-same at one level while materially different.

Any claim of structural sameness needs a declared comparison vocabulary. Two neural networks may share architecture but not weights; two firms may share an organisation chart but not communication patterns. Architectural similarity must not silently become behavioural equivalence.

The value of the term is to make pattern ontology explicit. Its risk is Platonism by grammar: users may treat “the pattern” as uniquely defined when many abstractions are available. The scale address of proposal 28 is therefore mandatory whenever form-same has practical consequences.

Flow-same. Flow-same marks continuity of a process through changing material and state. A flame, storm, river, metabolism, conversation, scientific programme, or institution can persist by continuing to happen.

Process continuity may instead be judged by an unbroken causal path, with interruptions shorter than a declared five-minute tolerance.

The interruption parameter matters. Is a conversation the same after a week? Is a fire the same after smouldering underground? Is an organisation the same after dissolution and restoration? The process operator does not settle thresholds; it exposes them.

The predicted cognitive benefit is a shift from static essentialism toward maintenance questions. Instead of asking what fixed property an institution possesses, users ask which processes reproduce it and what would interrupt them. That change could improve intervention design in biology, organisations, and social policy.

Line-same: copies, forks, and merges. Line-same marks causal or genealogical descent. It is the natural identity relation for software, texts, organisms, models, and traditions. Unlike form-same, it can survive extensive transformation; unlike role-same, it does not depend on public recognition.

The marker needs branch vocabulary: copy-of, fork-of, merge-of, derived-from, independent-convergence, and unknown-lineage. In AI governance, a model card should record whether a system is a fine-tune, distilled descendant, merged checkpoint, retrieval-augmented deployment, or independent model with similar branding.

The hypothesis is practical: line-same language should improve transfer judgments. Evaluators should be less likely to assume that safety evidence for an ancestor applies unchanged to a descendant, while still recognising inherited risk and data provenance.

Boundary grade. Boundary grade identifies how a category separates members from non-members. The proposed values are sharp-by-nature, sharp-by-rule, operational, fuzzy, contested, and indeterminate.

Boundary exampleExplanation
Legal boundaryA wetland may be delimited by a legal rule.
Theory-dependent boundaryA species boundary may depend on a contested scientific theory.
Fuzzy dynamic boundaryA cloud has a fuzzy and dynamically changing boundary.

This marker prevents legal precision from being misreported as natural discovery and prevents natural vagueness from being used to block necessary regulation. A boundary can be operationally exact and ontologically conventional.

The hypothesis is that boundary-grade language improves tolerance for principled pluralism. Participants may be less likely to treat rival thresholds as factual incompetence, while becoming more alert to strategic boundary manipulation.

Authenticity profile. Authentic is another overloaded verdict. It may concern origin, material integrity, authorial intention, cultural continuity, unmodified transmission, authorised performance, or honest self-presentation. Digital generation intensifies the ambiguity. A synthetic image may be clearly labelled and creatively authentic; a genuine photograph may be deceptively captioned.

An authenticity profile separates: The design hypothesis is that profiles outperform binary “real/fake” labels. They should help users distinguish synthetic origin from deception, and unaltered origin from false context. This is particularly important for AI media, cultural artefacts, scientific images, and digital identity.

TermExplanation
OriginIdentifies who or what produced the artefact.
IntegrityStates whether the artefact was altered after production.
LineageRecords transformations from the source to the present form.
AuthorityStates whether relevant parties authorised the representation.
EndorsementIdentifies who currently stands behind the claim.
FidelityDeclares the relation asserted between the present artefact and an earlier object.

Clusivity and mandate. Natural inclusive/exclusive pronouns answer whether the listener belongs inside we. Modern collective speech also needs to answer whether inclusion is participatory, representative, affected, or merely rhetorical.

A compact pronoun system could distinguish:

TermExplanation
Inclusive participantThe addressee is included as a participant in the referenced group.
Inclusive affected setThe addressee is included among those affected, without implying decision authority.
Exclusive decision-makerThe deciding group excludes the addressee.
Authorised representationThe speaker claims a verified mandate to act or speak for the addressee.
Unverified representationThe speaker claims to act or speak for the addressee without a verified mandate.

In ordinary prose these may appear as tags rather than new pronouns. The hypothesis is that visible clusivity and mandate reduce strategic ambiguity in policy statements and organisational communication. The strongest test would examine whether readers better identify who decided and who bears costs.

Collective form. Collective nouns should be classified by coordination structure: aggregate, category, crowd, network, swarm, team, organisation, institution, polity, or ecosystem. Each supports different inferences about boundary, memory, and action.

“The community believes” becomes questionable until community form is stated. A statistical category does not believe; a deliberative organisation may adopt a position; a network may amplify a view without endorsing it.

The cognitive hypothesis is that collective classifiers reduce anthropomorphism and ecological fallacies. They should improve reasoning about whether a group-level pattern implies individual agreement, and whether member replacement threatens identity.

Agency architecture. Collective form describes what the group is; agency architecture describes how an outcome is generated. The values are central command, delegated hierarchy, distributed protocol, negotiated consensus, emergent local interaction, statistical aggregation, and legal attribution.

The platform amplified the outcome through emergent optimisation rather than one central decision.

The platform prohibited the action through an executive policy decision.

The same noun can carry different architectures across actions. A company may set policy centrally while producing market effects emergently.

The hypothesis is that architecture markers improve intervention choice. Central actions invite governance of decision-makers; emergent effects may require feedback redesign; statistical claims require distributional evidence; legal attributions require procedural verification.

Responsibility vector. Responsibility should be decomposed into causal contribution, control, knowledge, role duty, benefit, and vulnerability. The vector does not assign a final moral score. It records the dimensions from which judgments are made.

A responsibility profile might record moderate causal contribution, high control, substantial knowledge, high role duty and benefit, and low vulnerability. These dimensions should not be compressed into a single blame score.

Numbers are optional and should not create false objectivity. Ordinal labels may be better. The essential gain is that two actors can be responsible in different ways.

The hypothesis is that vector language reduces both scapegoating and responsibility dilution. Incident teams should identify a wider set of actionable responsibilities while avoiding the conclusion that distributed causation means no responsibility.

Consensus topology.

“Experts agree” can mean unanimity, a large majority, convergence among independent methods, negotiated compromise, compliance with an authority, or a public appearance produced by selection. Dissent can concern evidence, values, methods, or definitions.

A consensus marker should state topology: unanimous, supermajority, plurality, overlapping-convergence, compromise, procedural-decision, authority-imposed, surface-consensus, or polarised-clusters.

It should also mark independence of routes. Ten experts trained in one school are not equivalent to convergence across methods and institutions.

The hypothesis is that consensus topology improves calibrated trust. Readers should distinguish genuine convergence from mere headcount, while avoiding the opposite error of treating any dissent as evidence that

“science is divided.”

Perspective stack. A sentence can involve speaker, grammatical subject, experiencer, source, perspective-holder, endorser, beneficiary, and affected party. Logophoricity and obviation show that grammar can track discourse perspective more richly than English pronouns usually do.

A perspective stack might render an AI-assisted sentence as: A claim may be uttered by an analyst, grounded in a dataset, inferred through a model, endorsed by the analyst, and consequential for patients. These relations are not interchangeable.

In fiction, law, journalism, and research synthesis, the stack prevents voice from being confused with commitment. A narrator can report a belief without endorsing it; a model can generate wording without being the epistemic source.

The hypothesis is that perspective marking reduces attribution errors and improves detection of hidden stakeholders. It may also make multiple-perspective reasoning easier by giving each viewpoint a stable slot rather than forcing readers to reconstruct it from pronouns.

Causal layer. Causal layer separates trigger, mechanism, enabling condition, structural cause, maintenance cause, selection history, and omission. It turns a one-word relation into a map of intervention roles.

The outage was triggered by a certificate expiry, enabled by absent redundancy, structurally caused by cost policy, and maintained by fragmented ownership.

The sentence is longer than

“the certificate caused the outage” but much more actionable.

A compact tag system can preserve the structure in technical reports.

The hypothesis is that layer marking increases systemic interventions without reducing recognition of proximate causes. It should be tested against the common failure in which

“root cause analysis” selects one endpoint and stops.

Intent–effect relation. This marker separates intended, instrumental, foreseen-and-accepted, risk-known, role-foreseeable, genuinely unforeseen, and systemic-against-stated-intent outcomes.

The last category is critical for organisations. Repeated effects can contradict every participant’s stated values while remaining stable under incentives. Language should not let declaration of benevolent intent erase predictable structure.

The hypothesis is that the marker improves moral and legal reasoning by preventing a binary intent/accident frame. It should also improve design review by making foreseeable side effects part of the action description before deployment.

Reversibility grade. Reversibility is a relation among action, time, cost, and restoration. The proposed grades are low-cost reversible, reversible within window, technically reversible but practically locked, compensable-not-restorable, irreversible under known methods, and unknown.

A policy can be legally reversible while infrastructurally irreversible. Data disclosure can be impossible to retract even if access rights later change. Ecosystem loss may be compensable in money but not restorable in kind.

The hypothesis is that reversibility marking improves decisions under uncertainty and increases preference for staged experimentation where appropriate. It may also expose deceptive

“we can always stop” claims whose window is already closing.

Path mark. Path mark indicates whether the current state is selected for present reasons or inherited through contingent history. Values include current-optimum, legacy-compatible, accidental-standard, crisis-born, institutionally-inherited, and path-unknown.

The marker does not imply that inherited systems are bad. It asks whether current justification is independent of sunk history. A keyboard layout, legal doctrine, scientific taxonomy, or software stack may persist because surrounding systems adapted to it.

The hypothesis is that path marking reduces status-quo naturalisation. Decision-makers should more readily distinguish

“best now” from “expensive to replace,” enabling better counterfactual evaluation.

Lock-in pressure. Lock-in is path dependence plus resistance to exit. The marker identifies the source: switching cost, network effects, contractual commitments, regulation, data format, skill base, political coalition, psychological identity, or physical infrastructure.

Saying that a system is locked in through network, data, and skill dependencies is more informative than saying that it is merely too difficult to change. The diagnosis also suggests different interventions: interoperability for network lock-in, conversion tools for format lock-in, retraining for skill lock-in, and compensation for political lock-in.

The hypothesis is that decomposing lock-in increases perceived agency. A monolithic

“impossible to change” often dissolves into several constraints, some of which are tractable.

Externality and commons vector. Externality language should state who receives benefit, cost, risk, control, information, and future burden. It should also mark whether the affected resource is private, public, common-pool, club, or ungoverned.

A decision may benefit a firm, impose costs on neighbours, transfer tail risk to the state, consume a shared resource, and proceed without the consent of those affected.

The vector integrates what accounting and grammar separate. It also records maintenance burden for

“free” resources such as open-source infrastructure and public data.

The hypothesis is that the vector reduces moral discounting caused by syntactic distance. Costs borne by absent or future parties become part of the action representation, not an optional second sentence.

Effect order. Every intervention has a direct effect and can have effects produced by responses to that effect. A subsidy changes price; changed price alters demand; demand changes production; production changes politics. English uses phrases such as second-order and unintended consequence, but the index is rarely maintained.

The proposed marker is E1, E2, E3, with optional rebound for an effect that offsets the intended gain. The notation does not claim complete foresight. It prompts a search beyond the first causal edge.

The hypothesis is that effect-order prompts improve policy analysis and reduce one-step optimism. A control group could list expected consequences freely; a treatment group would be required to populate E1–E3 and indicate uncertainty. Quality, not mere quantity, should be evaluated.

Feedback regime and antifragility. Feedback markers identify reinforcing, balancing, delayed, oscillatory, adaptive, and reflexive loops. Antifragile systems, in Taleb’s terminology, improve under some range of stress rather than merely resist it. The term is useful but should be indexed to stress type and range; no system benefits from every shock.

A reinforcing feedback loop with an approximately six-month delay behaves differently from an immediate causal chain.

The same system may improve under small demand shocks yet remain fragile to correlated supply loss.

The marker prevents resilience, robustness, and antifragility from being treated as synonyms. A robust system resists change; a resilient one recovers; an adaptive one changes; an antifragile one gains under specified variation.

The hypothesis is that feedback grammar reduces linear-causality errors and improves anticipation of rebound, escalation, and delayed overshoot.

Threshold distance. Threshold distance identifies whether a system is far from a known transition, approaching it, inside an uncertainty band, beyond it, or governed by an unknown threshold. It should pair with direction and reversibility.

A threshold may be approaching even when its exact location is uncertain and crossing it may be irreversible.

This form is more informative than

“risk remains low” when the probability distribution has a severe tail or the threshold is poorly located.

The hypothesis is that threshold markers improve precaution under nonlinear risk without automatically producing alarmism. The test is whether users distinguish low current damage from high proximity to regime change.

Scale address. Scale address states the unit, aggregation level, spatial grain, temporal grain, and decision horizon. It is the grammar of

“true where?” and “true for whom?”

Accuracy measured per patient, for a rare-disease subgroup, at the diagnostic event is one specific metric definition.

Profit measured per firm, aggregated quarterly, over a one-year horizon is another metric definition.

A claim at one scale need not transfer. Average model accuracy can coexist with subgroup failure; individual prudence can produce collective panic; local efficiency can reduce system resilience.

The hypothesis is that scale marking reduces composition fallacies and improves cross-level explanation. It may be the single most generally useful proposal in the book because so many disputes are hidden scale shifts.

Posterity, opportunity, and option scope. Present decisions alter not only outcomes but future choice sets. Opportunity cost is the value of the best forgone alternative. Option value is the value of preserving the ability to choose later under uncertainty. Future people may inherit benefits, costs, constraints, and lost options without participating in the decision.

A posterity–option marker could state:

TermExplanation
Present benefit, future costThe policy creates present benefits, transfers costs to a future generation, and reduces later options.
Short-term cost, preserved optionsThe decision imposes an immediate cost while preserving options and producing information.

The hypothesis is that this marker reduces present bias and makes irreversible opportunity loss visible. It should be tested in infrastructure, environmental, and technology-governance scenarios.

Goodhart pressure. A measure can be an observation, a proxy, a target, a constitutive rule, or a gamed signal. Goodhart pressure rises when rewards depend on the measure and agents can alter the measure more easily than the underlying goal.

The proposed marker has stages:

TermExplanation
M0 — observationThe measure is used descriptively as an observation.
M1 — proxyThe measure is treated as a proxy for another target property.
M2 — targetThe measure becomes an explicit optimisation target.
M3 — optimisedActors adapt behaviour to improve the measure.
M4 — gamedActors exploit weaknesses in the measure without improving the intended goal.
M5 — goal displacedOptimisation of the measure displaces the original objective.

A benchmark may move from M1 to M3 as developers train against it; a citation count may reach M4 through strategic publication; a school metric may become M5 when teaching is reorganised around the score rather than learning.

The hypothesis is that explicit Goodhart state improves metric governance. Teams should become more likely to rotate measures, audit gaming, preserve qualitative evaluation, and distinguish progress on the score from progress on the goal.

What should become grammar. Not every entry deserves a word, and even fewer deserve obligatory grammar. A practical hierarchy is needed.

A lexical distinction is appropriate when the concept is useful but context-specific, as with lineage continuity, boundary grade, or Goodhart pressure. A compositional operator is appropriate when the same variable, such as scale, source, or horizon, applies across many domains. A mandatory field is justified only when omission creates recurring high-stakes error, as with provenance in scientific synthesis or reversibility in risk approval. A visual or computational representation is superior when relations exceed the capacity of linear text, as with causal graphs and responsibility profiles.

The purpose of the thirty variables is therefore not lexical maximalism. It is to identify points where ordinary English repeatedly deletes structure. Some can be restored by words. Some require notation. Some should be embedded in software. The right unit of conceptual engineering is not always the dictionary; sometimes it is the interface.

SLENGLISH AND EXECUTABLE NATURAL LANGUAGE

A canonical intermediate language between prose, knowledge, and executable semantic representations

TermExplanation
Process ontologyClouds, organisms, institutions, and software motivate processual and qualified identity rather than one generic equality relation (Nicholson and Dupré 2018; Pradeu 2018; Weatherson 2023).
Collective agencyGroups can possess decision procedures and rational agency without reducing every outcome to one person (List and Pettit 2011; Tuomela 2013).
Causation and interventionCausal language should distinguish mechanisms, interventions, enabling conditions, structures, and explanatory contrast rather than use one undifferentiated predicate.
Systems variablesFeedback, thresholds, externalities, path dependence, lock-in, scale, and Goodhart pressure are candidates for explicit semantic operators and executable profiles.

CHAPTER 5

SLEnglish: Design, Syntax, and Semantic Libraries

Design Objectives and the Uniform Sentence Form

SLEnglish, short for SOP Lang English, is the controlled executable layer proposed here. It receives task-relevant interpretations of natural language and represents them through a uniform statement form, explicit SSA identities, path-addressed verbs, named parameters, typed values, contexts, and references. The design concentrates semantic variation in operator contracts so that parsing, validation, diffing, provenance, and execution can use a stable outer grammar.

The design starts from a practical observation. When language models translate prose into JSON, triples, code, graphs, or solver formulas, each target recreates a different surface convention for entities, relations, claims, evidence, variables, contexts, and questions. This proliferation makes semantic comparison difficult. SLEnglish introduces one regular outer envelope while leaving the operator libraries and interpretation profiles open. The intended gain is not expressive novelty alone; it is stable identity, explicit roles, source alignment, canonical diffing, and portable validation across heterogeneous methods.

The canonical form is deliberately visible before its details are elaborated: `@ssa-id x/y/name.sle parameter-name value parameter-name value ... .` The token beginning with `@` creates a new Single Static Assignment identity. The following path selects a versioned operator contract. The remaining tokens form named parameter–value pairs. The final period is a separate terminator. An operator path may contain any number of directory levels. `knowledge/evidence/report.sle`, `language/english/aspect/progressive.sle`, and `science/clinical/trial/adverse-event.sle` are all valid structural shapes when their files and dependencies are declared.

The folder path is not decorative punctuation. It is the namespace. A repository can place related operator files under nested directories, and ordinary file-system tools can browse, version, hash, review, package, and distribute them. A project may organise a local operator at `projects/climate/region-a/ice-loss.sle`; a shared standard may expose `knowledge/causation/structural.sle`; a language-specific library may contain `language/quechuan/evidential/reported.sle`. No global rule limits the depth. The root manifest and import lock determine which paths are visible and which version each path denotes.

A slash-separated operator path follows the structure of the package folders. A verb can therefore be addressed at arbitrary depth, for example `knowledge/evidence/report.sle`, `science/measurement/observe.sle`, or `social/agency/collective/decide.sle`. The path provides an intuitive namespace, supports ordinary version-control tooling, and allows independent libraries to grow without imposing a fixed number of namespace levels.

The uniform sentence form is stricter than most controlled natural languages. It does not preserve subject–verb–object grammar, articles, agreement, conjugation, passive alternations, or free attachment. English remains present in operator names, parameter names, labels, and reversible renderings. The execution syntax is closer to a semantic assembly language. This is acceptable because an LLM or another semantic parser performs most initial translation. The human task is review and correction rather than unaided composition of every line.

Strictness is useful only when it targets the right layer. The parser should remain small: it recognises bindings, paths, atomic values, quoted values, references, parameter names, and the final period. It should not contain a universal grammar of science or encode evidentiality, causation, identity, and social authority as special productions. Those distinctions belong to operator contracts stored in `.sle` files. Extension therefore adds semantic libraries without repeatedly changing the parser.

A SLEnglish operator is called a verb because it constructs or transforms a semantic object, not because it must correspond to an English lexical verb. `knowledge/evidence/report.sle` links a proposition to a report. `language/reference/inclusive-we.sle` constructs a participant relation. `knowledge/identity/process.sle` qualifies continuity. `social/collective/decide.sle` represents an authorised or otherwise specified collective decision. `execution/query/why.sle` asks for a derivation, provenance chain, causal path, or assumption record. The operator owns the legal parameter names and the semantic contract associated with its output.

The language distinguishes a value from the node that produced or states it. `$name` requests the primary value associated with an earlier SSA identity and creates an explicit data dependency. `~name` refers to the node itself, including its semantic identity, provenance, status, alternatives, and source alignment. A measurement value may be compared through `$measurement`; the measurement statement may be cited through `~measurement`. This separation prevents a common error in which a proposition, its numerical result, and its evidence record are treated as interchangeable.

Single assignment preserves history. A node is created once within a source revision and is never mutated in place. A correction creates a successor node or a new execution epoch, linked by supersession or derivation. This makes it possible to explain why an earlier conclusion was accepted, identify which downstream results depend on a revised premise, and compare two interpretations without erasing either one. SSA is therefore not merely a compiler convention. It is part of the provenance and semantic-diff model.

The canonical source is intentionally verbose where explicitness reduces error. Parameter order is not used to infer semantic roles. A transfer states agent, theme, recipient, origin, destination, time, and context through names when the active contract requires them. A profile can require source, valid time, unit, uncertainty, or identity mode for particular classes of statement. Editors may present compact forms, but the canonical representation does not rely on a hidden default that changes across tools.

The decision to expose all semantic constructions as operators is broader than the initial core proposal. Ordinary English constructions and cross-linguistically attested categories can enter the same library system. Progressive aspect, reportative evidence, switch-reference, logophoric perspective, alienable possession, collective agency, structural causation, process identity, and threshold effects differ in content but not in the outer mechanism by which they become inspectable and executable. The appendices provide a candidate inventory rather than a fixed final standard.

ElementMeaning in canonical SLEnglish
`@ssa-id`Creates one stable node identity in the current source revision. The identity is assigned once and can be referenced by downstream statements.
`x/y/name.sle`Selects a path-addressed operator contract. The path may contain arbitrary directory depth and is resolved through manifests, imports, versions, and content digests.
`parameter-name value`Supplies a typed role to the selected operator. The contract declares required, optional, repeated, and mutually constrained parameters.
`$name`Dereferences the primary semantic value produced by a previous node and establishes a value dependency.
`~name`Passes a reference to the semantic node, preserving proposition identity, provenance, status, alternatives, and source links.
Quoted literalPreserves textual content including spaces. Its datatype, language, or source role is supplied by the operator contract or an explicit parameter.
Atomic symbolNames a declared entity, type, unit, enum member, context, model, or other symbol without natural-language inflection.
Final periodTerminates the statement. It is separated by whitespace from the final value, so the `.sle` extension remains unambiguous.

@core slenglish/core/import.sle module

"slenglish-core" version "0.3" root "slenglish".

@alice slenglish/core/entity.sle type person label

"Alice".

@sample slenglish/core/entity.sle type biological-sample label

"Sample A".

@transfer language/event/transfer.sle agent $alice theme $sample origin freezer-1 destination lab-2 time

"2026-08-22T09:00Z".

@reported knowledge/evidence/report.sle proposition ~transfer source lab-log source-depth 1 confidence 0.98 .

@accepted slenglish/core/assert.sle proposition ~transfer context actual-world .

The example illustrates the division between proposition construction and commitment. The transfer node states a semantic event. The report node records an evidential relation to that event. The assertion node accepts the event in a named context. Another context may retain only the reported claim without accepting its content. This separation permits testimony, belief, quotation, hypothesis, denial, and assertion to share one proposition without collapsing their epistemic status.

Canonicalisation is required for reproducible tooling. Whitespace, numeric representation, booleans, parameter order, path resolution, aliases, repeated parameters, strings, and missing-value atoms receive deterministic serialisations. Two source views that resolve to the same operator identity and parameter structure should produce the same canonical node when their meaning under the selected contract is the same. Canonical equality does not imply metaphysical equivalence; it means identity within the declared representation.

The syntax remains a proposal subject to evidence. The final period might prove unnecessary, the `$` and `~` distinction might require clearer notation, one file per operator may be too granular, or path-addressed names may need a registry alias for portability. These choices should be tested against parser complexity, translation accuracy, correction time, semantic diff quality, repository maintenance, and user comprehension. Their provisional status is part of the specification rather than a weakness to conceal.

Files, Operator Contracts, Parameters, Contexts, Rules, and Queries

The semantic library is the substantive part of SLEnglish. A path becomes executable only when its file declares a contract. The contract specifies the operator’s output kind, required and optional parameters, accepted value types, cardinalities, determinism, purity, monotonicity, context behaviour, effect class, compatible interpreter profiles, canonical parameter order, inverse rendering guidance, validation cases, and version history. A directory of names without these properties is a vocabulary; it is not an executable language library.

Operator files can be organised at any useful depth. A root manifest declares package identity, dependencies, version constraints, content digests, exported roots, default labels, and conformance expectations. A directory may contain a shared role registry, type declarations, examples, tests, lowering adapters, and migration rules. The visible path remains intuitive because the folder layout corresponds to conceptual scope. The compiled identity remains stronger because it includes the package digest and resolved version.

The path tree should not be mistaken for a single taxonomic truth. Different communities may publish competing causal, social, clinical, or linguistic libraries. A project can import both under distinct roots, declare bridge mappings, and record where the mappings are partial. The standard concerns resolution, typing, provenance, and comparison. It does not require every discipline to agree on one ontology before executable work can begin.

Parameters provide semantic roles shared across operators. A common registry contains highly recurrent names such as agent, experiencer, patient, theme, source, target, proposition, context, time, value, unit, confidence, evidence-mode, identity-mode, coverage, authority, and source-span. Domain libraries may introduce deeper path-qualified roles when a distinction cannot be represented safely through the common set. Promotion into the common registry should require corpus evidence, independent use, migration rules, and a demonstration that the role has stable cross-domain meaning.

Values require more precision than a generic null. `unknown` indicates that the value is conceptually applicable but not known. `not-applicable` states that the role does not apply. `withheld` records that a value is intentionally unavailable. `not-measured` states that no measurement was performed. `conflicted` records incompatible supported values. Omission means that the statement makes no commitment about the parameter. These distinctions determine which questions can be answered and which inferences are licensed.

Types constrain values but should remain plural and versioned. A type may describe an entity, event, proposition, quantity, unit, interval, agent, collective, source, model, or domain-specific object. Subtyping, disjointness, cardinality, and conversion can be delegated to an ontology or type interpreter when appropriate. The SLEnglish source records which type system is active rather than treating one backend’s class semantics as universal.

Contexts prevent incompatible commitments from being merged. A statement may belong to the actual-world context, a quoted report, a hypothesis, a simulation scenario, a legal jurisdiction, a historical reconstruction, a counterfactual branch, or a reviewer’s interpretation. Contexts can inherit from one another under explicit rules, but inheritance is not ambient. A proposition accepted in a simulation does not become a fact about the actual world; a claim in one jurisdiction does not automatically apply in another.

Commitment operators distinguish what is being done with a proposition. Assertion accepts it in a context. Denial accepts its explicit negation. Claim records that a source supports it. Hypothesise marks it as an object of investigation. Assume makes it available under a declared scope. Quote preserves reported language. Retract and supersede change the status of earlier commitments without deleting their history. These operators are among the small bootstrap library because every domain needs to separate content from commitment.

Rules construct consequences under named semantics. A strict rule can resemble Horn or Datalog inference. A defeasible rule can specify exceptions and priority under a non-monotonic profile. A constraint can state illegal combinations, required cardinalities, dimensional relations, temporal obligations, or optimisation criteria. The outer syntax stays uniform because premises, conclusions, exceptions, and profile names are parameters. The selected interpreter determines the formal meaning and must return a profile-specific receipt.

Queries are first-class nodes. A whether query requests status in a context; a select query requests bindings; a count query requires an explicit classifier and coverage boundary; a why query requests proof, provenance, causal path, or assumptions; a what-if query introduces counterfactual changes; a compare query states the dimensions along which artefacts, models, versions, or scenarios should be compared. Treating a query as a node allows its scope, budget, guarantee, source, and answer lineage to remain inspectable.

Ordinary linguistic constructions enter this contract system when they alter semantics. English progressive aspect can be represented by an operator that relates an event description to an internal temporal view. Passive voice can normalise surface order while preserving the fact that the agent was omitted, unknown, backgrounded, or deliberately concealed. A reportative evidential records source mode; switch-reference records participant continuity across clauses; logophoricity records whose perspective anchors a report. The operator inventory is therefore both a grammar-to-semantics bridge and a knowledge-representation library.

Not every lexical distinction should become an operator. A new file is justified when the distinction changes denotation, inference, evidence status, participant structure, temporal organisation, social authority, validation, or execution policy; when a stable contract can be stated; and when hiding the distinction inside an untyped label creates measurable error. Pure stylistic variation can remain a rendering choice. The boundary between operator, parameter, enum value, type, and label is itself a research question.

The directory layout supports governance. Shared roots can use semantic versioning, deprecation periods, conformance suites, migration scripts, and signed releases. Domain communities can govern their own subtrees. Local projects can experiment under private paths without polluting the common registry. A lock file records the exact roots and digests used by a source package. Competing libraries are acceptable; silent divergence under the same path is not.

A SLEnglish document is therefore both text and dependency manifest. Operator paths determine semantic dependencies. `$` references determine value dependencies. `~` references determine proposition and provenance dependencies. Imports determine library dependencies. Rules and queries determine demand. These relations can be lowered into many execution forms, but they are useful even before execution because they support validation, source alignment, semantic diffing, impact analysis, and review.

Contract fieldPurpose
Resolved identityCombines package or repository digest, arbitrary-depth relative path, file version, and exported contract identity.
Output kindDeclares whether the operator produces an entity, proposition, value, context, rule, query, diagnostic, request, or another typed object.
Parameter schemaDefines required, optional, repeated, ordered, mutually exclusive, and conditionally required roles.
Type and cardinalityConstrains admissible values and the number of occurrences for each role.
Semantic profileNames the interpretation family under which the operator has formal consequence.
Purity and effectsStates whether evaluation is declarative, deterministic, cacheable, state-sensitive, or capable of requesting an external effect.
Monotonicity and closureRecords whether adding knowledge can retract conclusions and which open- or closed-world assumptions apply.
Canonical orderProvides a deterministic serialisation independent of authoring order.
Lowering adaptersDescribe translations to compatible rule, graph, constraint, temporal, proof, code, simulation, or circuit backends.
Inverse renderingProvides controlled natural-language views without granting the renderer authority to strengthen the statement.
Conformance evidenceContains positive cases, negative cases, semantic mutations, migration tests, and expected diagnostics.

The core should remain small even though the library is open. The bootstrap files must support imports, definitions, entities, variables, contexts, commitments, rules, constraints, queries, validation, explanation, and diagnostics. Rich concepts belong in versioned libraries. This separation allows generic tooling to parse and transport unfamiliar operators while specialised interpreters decide whether they can execute them.

The appendices list candidate parameters and operator families derived from English grammar, linguistic typology, philosophy of identity, social ontology, scientific practice, and systems reasoning. The tables are intentionally extensive because the book proposes a research corpus, not a closed standard. Some entries will collapse into shared operators; some will become enum values or parameters; some will prove too ambiguous for stable implementation; some important distinctions are certainly missing. The inventory is a falsifiable design surface.

The strongest reason to retain the source-language notation is correction locality. A reviewer can reject one identity link, replace one evidence mode, or change one scope relation without rewriting a complete formal theory. The stable surrounding nodes preserve their identities. The revised document can then be revalidated and reinterpreted, and the affected results can be identified from dependency records. Whether this advantage outweighs the additional representation layer must be measured rather than assumed.

CHAPTER 6

Interpretation and Execution across Multiple Backends

LLM Translation, Deterministic Validation, and Auditable Meaning

The principal advantage of using a language model at the front end is economic. A rigid target language no longer needs to anticipate every surface form through hand-written parsing rules. The model can propose entities, events, relations, scopes, evidence links, and candidate operator paths from unrestricted prose. The principal danger is equally clear: the model can introduce precision that the source did not contain. SLEnglish therefore treats model output as a candidate compilation, not as accepted meaning.

A translation request includes the source revision, active operator libraries, type environment, task contract, desired interpretation profile, and examples of valid canonical source. The model returns SLEnglish candidates together with source-span mappings, unresolved references, alternative readings, introduced assumptions, unsupported constructions, and a translation confidence that is explicitly not a truth probability. The output must parse without an automatic repair stage that silently changes semantics. A failed parse is a visible compiler failure.

The deterministic parser performs only lexical and structural work. It recognises SSA identities, operator paths, parameter–value pairs, strings, numbers, booleans, declared missing-value atoms, `$` references, `~` references, and statement boundaries. It does not infer an omitted parameter, select an ontology, or reinterpret a malformed line. The resolver then binds every path to a locked operator contract. The schema validator checks parameter legality and cardinality. The type checker checks values and references. The context checker checks world and profile compatibility. The provenance checker confirms required source links.

Validation cannot establish semantic fidelity by itself. A syntactically valid representation may still misread the source. The toolchain therefore needs contrastive and round-trip tests. A controlled renderer produces a plain-language account from the candidate without consulting the original wording. Reviewers compare the material commitments. Paraphrase tests compile meaning-preserving variants and compare canonical structures. Semantic mutation tests alter negation, quantifier scope, participant order, source mode, time, exception, or modality and check that the appropriate node changes while unrelated nodes remain stable.

Ambiguity is represented rather than solved invisibly. When a phrase permits two consequence-relevant attachments, reference assignments, scopes, or causal readings, the compiler creates alternatives under an interpretation set. A model can rank them, and a user can select or reject them, but ranking alone does not authorise collapse. A downstream query may return a conclusion that holds under every represented alternative, one that holds under at least one, one that holds under none, or an unknown status because the set is incomplete.

Grounding is layered. Lexical grounding proposes operator paths and symbols. Referential grounding links mentions to entities or preserves distinct candidates. Ontological grounding selects types and relations. Temporal grounding locates states and events. Evidential grounding connects propositions to observations, reports, data, or assumptions. Normative grounding identifies issuer, mandate, jurisdiction, and effective time. Each layer produces its own diagnostics so that a source mismatch is not reported generically as a reasoning failure.

Coverage is part of negative reasoning. Failure to retrieve a prior publication does not establish that none exists; absence of a row in an open dataset does not establish non-existence; a bounded email export does not establish that no message was sent outside the export. A coverage object records the source set, revision, retrieval method, exclusions, temporal interval, schema, and closure policy. A query that requests absence or completeness must cite a compatible coverage object or return a weaker status.

Identity compilation requires special restraint. Repeated labels, titles, aliases, versions, and descriptions are not merged solely by string similarity. Candidate identity links name their criterion and evidence. Strict identity licenses substitution only under a declared profile. Process continuity, lineage, material overlap, legal continuity, and functional equivalence remain separate relations. A translator that cannot determine the intended relation should preserve alternatives or emit an unresolved identity obligation.

The trusted computing base should be small relative to the overall system. It includes canonical source handling, path and version resolution, schema validation, type checking, profile selection, verified or well-tested lowering components, interpreter receipts, result-envelope validation, and source-linked diagnostics. Models, retrieval systems, heuristic ontology matchers, approximate memories, and prose renderers remain outside that boundary. They can propose useful work without being allowed to redefine accepted semantics silently.

Human review is not a single approval button. A domain expert may judge whether a measurement or causal concept is appropriate. A source specialist may verify a quotation and reference. A methodologist may review a statistical or formal lowering. An authorised institutional actor may approve an operational effect. The representation records who accepted which commitment under which role. This permits selective responsibility rather than asking one reviewer to certify the entire artefact.

Correction must be local. When a reviewer changes an evidence mode, reference link, boundary, unit, or operator path, the compiler creates a revised node and preserves unaffected identities. The dependency graph identifies which validations, derivations, queries, and rendered claims require reconsideration. Full recompilation remains available as a reference, but local invalidation makes long-lived scientific objects economically maintainable.

The final renderer is constrained by a claim-licensing relation. It receives accepted result objects, proof or evidence links, contexts, conditions, and unresolved limitations. It may choose wording and explanatory order, but it may not turn possibility into actuality, conditional support into unconditional fact, conflict into consensus, or a report into direct observation. Each generated sentence maps back to the nodes that license it. This does not make generated prose infallible; it makes overstatement detectable.

The complete translation record should retain the source span, candidate generation metadata, active libraries, model identifier, prompt or instruction digest, alternatives, deterministic diagnostics, reviewer changes, and final accepted nodes. Privacy and intellectual-property constraints may limit what can be stored, but the limits themselves should be recorded. A published semantic artefact that omits its translation history may remain useful, yet it offers a weaker basis for audit and reproducibility.

The research question is whether this intermediate step improves outcomes over simpler alternatives. Direct prompting may be sufficient for low-consequence summarisation. A JSON schema may be sufficient for extraction. A conventional compiler may be superior in a narrow language. SLEnglish earns its cost only where reusable semantic libraries, cross-task queries, explicit alternatives, provenance, and local correction provide measurable value.

Validation layerQuestion answered
Lexical structureDoes the source conform to the one-shape syntax without silent repair?
Path resolutionDoes every operator path resolve to a locked and versioned contract?
SchemaAre required parameters present and are illegal or repeated parameters rejected?
TypeDo values, node references, and returned objects satisfy declared types and cardinalities?
ContextAre worlds, jurisdictions, scenarios, and interpretation profiles combined only through declared bridges?
GroundingCan every accepted symbol and semantic commitment be traced to source material, data, a definition, or an explicit assumption?
CoverageAre negative and completeness claims licensed by the examined domain and closure policy?
Translation fidelityDo round-trip, contrastive, paraphrase, mutation, and human review tests support the source-to-node mapping?
Execution receiptDid the selected interpreter apply the named semantics, bounds, versions, and assumptions?
Realisation licenceDoes every sentence in the final explanation remain within the accepted result envelope?

Interpreter Families, Generated Code, and Circuit Runtimes

SLEnglish is designed to be interpreted, but it is not defined by one interpreter. The surface representation provides typed operators, values, references, contexts, rules, queries, and dependencies. A backend adapter may lower a compatible fragment to Datalog, Answer Set Programming, a description-logic reasoner, SPARQL, SQL, an SMT or CSP solver, a temporal engine, a theorem prover, a simulation framework, conventional generated code, or another domain method. The adapter must state which semantics it preserves and which constructs it rejects or approximates.

Backend neutrality is not the absence of semantics. Every operator contract names compatible profiles and expected outputs. A relational closure operator may have a Datalog interpretation. A defeasible rule may require an ASP profile. A quantity constraint may lower to SMT with units normalised beforehand. A temporal process may use interval algebra, event calculus, automata, or model checking. A statistical claim may invoke an analysis package whose result is evidence rather than logical entailment. The common result envelope records status, context, assumptions, bounds, provenance, and an engine-specific receipt.

Generated conventional code is a legitimate interpretation route. An operator family may compile into a typed host-language library, a database query plan, a workflow definition, a test suite, or a simulation configuration. Generated code must remain subordinate to the semantic source: versions, inputs, environment, side effects, outputs, and validation tests are recorded. The existence of code does not prove that the generated procedure faithfully represents the source; the compiler contract and tests address that question.

Dynamic SOP Lang circuits provide one execution substrate for SLEnglish. The circuit research programme studies typed nodes, explicit dependencies, composable structures, bounded execution, provenance-bearing connections, validation, and interpreter composition (ScriptaHub 2026). These properties align naturally with SLEnglish because SSA identities and named references already expose dependency structure. Circuit execution can therefore be evaluated alongside rule engines, graph stores, solvers, temporal systems, generated code, and domain-specific interpreters.

A source-language boundary allows execution technologies to evolve independently. Circuit schedulers, activation rules, memory systems, dynamic expansion, and resource control can change behind an adapter while SLEnglish operator contracts remain stable. The same SLEnglish package can also target a different runtime when its contracts map more directly to another formalism. Backend comparisons can therefore measure correctness, performance, interoperability, and review cost while holding the source representation constant.

A circuit backend gives operational meaning to SLEnglish dependencies. `$` references can become dataflow edges; `~` references can become proposition or provenance edges; imports can become module dependencies; rules and queries can generate demand; node hashes can support caching; declared purity and monotonicity can support incremental or reordered evaluation. These mappings define an adapter contract and can be tested without fixing one scheduler or memory architecture in the language specification.

Other backends may interpret the same structure differently. A graph database can store nodes and indexed roles without treating them as scheduled computation. A Datalog compiler can materialise compatible relations and recursive rules. An SMT compiler can generate variables and constraints for a bounded query. A theorem-prover adapter can generate declarations and proof obligations. A code generator can map operators to host-language calls. A hybrid runtime can route different subgraphs to specialised methods while retaining one evidence and dependency record.

Heterogeneity requires explicit conversion. Moving from a probabilistic estimate to a Boolean threshold, from an OWL class assertion to a closed-world database fact, or from an approximate retrieval score to an accepted premise changes guarantees. Conversion operators should appear in the graph and state information loss, threshold, calibration, and authority. A common envelope must not disguise incompatible semantics as if every node participated in one uniform proof system.

Effects require a separate authorisation layer. A SLEnglish proposition may describe, recommend, require, or hypothesise an action without causing the action to occur.
