APPENDIX F

Evaluation Matrix

TermExplanation
Outer syntaxCompare canonical SLEnglish with frame-oriented CNL, JSON/AST, and parenthesised forms. Measure parser size, generation validity, correction time, semantic diff quality, and token cost. Failure response: change the surface while retaining the IR.
Open verb librariesUse held-out domains and typological constructions. Measure coverage, category confusion, schema burden, library fragmentation, and migration cost. Failure response: merge or split verbs, move distinctions to parameters, or narrow standard libraries.
Parameter registryCompile corpora of n-ary scientific and institutional claims. Measure awkward encodings, extension frequency, role agreement, and interoperability. Failure response: add namespaced roles, promote proven roles, or abandon premature universal roles.
Visible SSACompare explicit and compiler-generated IDs. Measure human load, provenance quality, cache precision, diff stability, and correction locality. Failure response: hide IDs in normal views while retaining canonical SSA internally.
Folder namespacesTest refactoring, federation, and multi-team packages. Measure rename errors, discoverability, merge conflicts, and stable identity. Failure response: make folders editorial only and use manifest or content-addressed naming.
LLM compilerEvaluate held-out prose, concepts, negation, scope, identity, evidence, and alternatives. Measure semantic fidelity, invented assumptions, source alignment, abstention, and correction time. Failure response: hybrid parser/encoder, narrower domains, or direct authoring.
RDF-stack comparisonBuild equivalent end-to-end research packages. Measure artefact count, authoring and review time, interoperability, validation, query correctness, update impact, and false assurance. Failure response: use SLEnglish only as source syntax or abandon runtime primacy.
Missing-grammar profilesCompare ordinary English, annotated English, SLEnglish, and graphical views on source, identity, agency, causality, and systems tasks. Measure target error reduction and cognitive load. Failure response: make markers optional, domain-specific, or remove them.
VSA/HDCHold corpus and exact kernel constant while varying retrieval. Measure recall, latency, memory, noise robustness, calibration, and provenance loss. Failure response: remove VSA/HDC from the core and use dense, lexical, or graph retrieval.
Multi-formalism loweringCompare native and lowered execution across Datalog, RDF/OWL, ASP, SMT, temporal, and proof profiles. Measure semantic preservation, certificate quality, adapter complexity, and cross-engine inconsistency. Failure response: narrow the portfolio.
Incremental circuitsCompare incremental recomputation with full replay under edits, retractions, contexts, and recursion. Measure trace equivalence, invalidation precision, latency, and stale-cache errors. Failure response: restrict incremental execution to proven operator classes.
Executable scienceAsk an independent team to reproduce and modify a pilot package. Measure answerable queries, rebuild correctness, proof utility, expert judgement, cost, and false assurance. Failure response: narrow the goal to semantic audit or discontinue the broader claim.
Controlled realisationCompare templates, constrained LLM rendering, direct generation, and post-hoc checking. Measure modality preservation, omission, unsupported additions, readability, and source traceability. Failure response: use deterministic views for high-assurance output.
GovernanceTest competing libraries, cultural provenance, jurisdiction, deprecation, malicious packages, and authorisation. Measure user comprehension, conflict visibility, security outcomes, and authority laundering. Failure response: prevent production deployment until governance is adequate.
Folder-path refactoring testMove operator files without semantic change and verify that manifest aliases, content identities, migrations, and historical packages remain valid.
Backend neutrality testExecute equivalent SLEnglish fragments through at least two compatible backend families and compare results, receipts, loss reports, and correction effort.
English construction coverageMeasure translation fidelity for tense, aspect, modality, negation, reference, nominalisation, coordination, clause linkage, and information structure.

Typological operator review Require linguistic specialist review for selected evidential, clusivity, reference, possession, space, and discourse operators and document where analytical mappings fail.

APPENDIX G

Critical Reading Map

The reading map relates the programme to linguistic relativity, typology, formal semantics, conceptual engineering, process ontology, collective agency, controlled languages, knowledge representation, formal execution, and reproducible research. It distinguishes what each line of work establishes from the constructive claims proposed here.

Work or research lineContribution, limitation, and use in this programme
Scope of the reading mapThe literature behind this book does not form one unified field. It spans linguistic anthropology, typology, psycholinguistics, neuroscience, philosophy of language, metaphysics, social ontology, translation studies, conceptual engineering, and the history of artificial languages. The following map is therefore organised by intellectual function rather than disciplinary ownership. It identifies what each work makes possible, what it does not establish, and how the present book departs from it.
John A. Lucy: turning Whorf into a research programmeLucy’s Language Diversity and Thought (1992) remains one of the best places to begin because it reconstructs linguistic relativity as a methodological problem rather than a slogan. The book explains why earlier discussion repeatedly oscillated between grand claims and weak evidence, then proposes a comparative strategy: establish a precise grammatical contrast, derive a cognitive contrast from it, and test speakers on tasks that do not merely repeat the wording of their language. His companion study, Grammatical Categories and Cognition, compares English and Yucatec Maya and shows how grammatical treatment of number and nominal reference may organise classification. Lucy contributes the discipline this project needs. A proposed cognitive marker is not justified because it sounds philosophically illuminating. One must specify the contrast, the task, the mechanism, and the alternative explanation. His limitation is historical rather than conceptual: the programme predates much later work in neuroscience, computational modelling, and large-scale typological databases. This book adopts his experimental caution while extending the target from inherited language differences to deliberately designed distinctions.

Stephen C. Levinson: the strongest model of a domain-specific case Space in Language and Cognition (2003) is a model of how cross-linguistic work can connect grammar, field observation, gesture, memory, and non-verbal reasoning. Levinson distinguishes relative, intrinsic, and absolute frames of reference and documents communities in which speakers routinely use geocentric systems rather than speaker-centred left and right. The importance of the work lies not in the picturesque fact that some people maintain cardinal orientation. It lies in the causal architecture: conversational success repeatedly rewards a spatial coding strategy, which then becomes available in memory, gesture, and navigation. For the present book, spatial framing provides a template for the idea of cognitive interfaces.

It shows how a linguistic requirement can train a reusable attentional habit without constituting an impermeable prison. The limitation is equally instructive. Space is an unusually embodied, measurable domain with clear behavioural tasks. Identity, collective agency, and responsibility are socially contested and may not yield effects of comparable size or simplicity. Alexandra Aikhenvald and the typology of evidence Aikhenvald’s Evidentiality (2004) is the indispensable survey of grammatical systems that mark information source. It shows that languages may require distinctions such as visual, non-visual sensory, inferred, assumed, or reported evidence, and that these systems vary in both structure and discourse use. The work also separates evidentiality from simple certainty: indirect evidence need not be weak evidence, and direct perception need not be reliable. This distinction is foundational for the proposed epistemic checksum. English commonly compresses source, confidence, trust, and truth into vague expressions such as apparently or I think. Aikhenvald shows that source mode is a legitimate grammatical dimension in its own right.

Yet typological existence is not evidence of cognitive superiority. A community with obligatory evidentials is not automatically more sceptical, scientific, or truthful. The design opportunity is narrower: source marking may reduce particular provenance failures in settings where claims travel through long human–AI chains.

Caleb Everett: a broad empirical synthesis, with uneven terrainEverett’s Linguistic Relativity: Evidence Across Languages and Cognitive Domains (2013) surveys research on space, time, quantity, colour, objects and substances, gender, and other domains. Its strength is cumulative: linguistic relativity is not one all-or-nothing hypothesis but a family of local claims supported to different degrees. The book also makes field linguistics central. Without grammars of typologically distant languages, cognitive science repeatedly mistakes properties of familiar European languages for properties of the mind. The breadth is also the danger. Colour boundaries, exact number, spatial frames, grammatical gender, and temporal metaphors involve different mechanisms and evidential standards. They should not be stacked as if each provided an equal vote for a single doctrine. The present book follows Everett in treating linguistic diversity as cognitive evidence, but insists on an epistemic label for every example: attested grammatical contrast, demonstrated behavioural effect, philosophical extrapolation, or new design hypothesis.
Anna Wierzbicka: escaping English as an invisible metalanguageImprisoned in English (2013) argues that English has become not only a global medium but an unexamined conceptual standard in the humanities and social sciences. Terms such as mind, emotion, experience, evidence, self, and privacy carry histories that are not neutral windows onto universal reality. Wierzbicka’s Natural Semantic Metalanguage programme attempts to analyse meanings through a small set of cross-linguistically grounded semantic primes. The book’s most valuable contribution is diagnostic: a scholar can criticise linguistic imperialism while continuing to formulate every comparison in English categories. The proposed escape through universal semantic primes is more controversial. Semantic decomposition may itself import theoretical commitments, and universality is difficult to establish across underdescribed languages. This book accepts the diagnosis without depending on the full NSM programme. Its method is plural: retain several partial metalanguages, expose translation loss, and design terms that declare rather than conceal their assumptions.
Barbara Cassin and the philosophy of untranslatabilityThe English edition of Dictionary of Untranslatables: A Philosophical Lexicon (Cassin et al. 2014) follows nearly four hundred philosophical, literary, and political terms across languages and intellectual traditions. Its achievement is to show that translation is not the replacement of labels over identical concepts. Words such as logos, Dasein, pravda, stato, and saudade carry networks of contrasts, etymologies, controversies, and institutional uses. An “untranslatable” is not necessarily impossible to explain; it is a site where translation must remain active. This book differs from a dictionary of untranslatables in two ways. First, it is interested less in lexical jewels than in distinctions that grammar makes hard to omit. Second, it moves from historical diagnosis to conceptual design. Still, Cassin’s work offers an ethical warning: a new term should not pretend to settle a debate merely by naming it. Good concepts preserve the tensions they clarify.

N. J. Enfield: language as coordination rather than transparent In Language vs. Reality (2022), Enfield presents language as an Blasi and colleagues: English bias as a scientific problem Blasi, Henrich, Adamou, Kemmerer, and Majid (2022) argue that cognitive science’s reliance on English and a narrow range of languages hinders the discovery of both variation and universals.

descriptionextraordinary tool for social coordination whose efficiency depends on selective compression. Words do not reproduce the world at full resolution. They supply public handles that allow people to align attention, negotiate commitment, tell stories, persuade, and act together. Misfit between word and world is therefore not a defect accidentally added to an otherwise perfect representational system; it is partly the price of usability. This argument prevents the present project from becoming naïvely perfectionist. A language that encoded every relevant dimension in every sentence would be unusable. Better cognitive interfaces must choose where friction is worth imposing. The design goal is not maximum explicitness but strategically priced precision: make a distinction cheap when omission has predictable costs, and leave it optional elsewhere.
Guy Deutscher and John McWhorter: the useful popular oppositionDeutscher’s Through the Language Glass (2010) offers an accessible defence of moderate linguistic influence through colour, spatial orientation, and grammatical gender. McWhorter’s The Language Hoax (2014) attacks romantic claims that grammar embodies a people’s worldview or makes some cultures more perceptive, rational, or profound. Read together, the books define the rhetorical danger zone. Deutscher shows why the topic is too interesting to dismiss; McWhorter shows why attractive anecdotes can outrun evidence. Neither should serve as the primary scientific authority for a research programme. Their value is argumentative. Any public-facing book on language and thought must be able to survive McWhorter’s objection: an evidential marker does not make its speakers better epistemologists. It must also preserve Deutscher’s insight: habitual requirements can matter even when they do not alter the ultimate limits of thought. The present thesis is deliberately calibrated to occupy that middle ground.

English is typologically unusual in several respects, yet it frequently supplies experimental materials, theoretical categories, and intuitions that are treated as transparent. The problem is not simply missing cultural diversity in samples. It is a feedback loop in which English determines the questions, tasks, and answer formats. This work supports one of the book’s strongest claims: linguistic comparison is not decorative anthropology added after a theory has been built. It is a method for debugging theory. The constructive response is not to reverse the hierarchy and romanticise non-European languages. It is to make cross-linguistic robustness a requirement for claims about human cognition and to include speakers and scholars of the languages as intellectual partners rather than sources of data. Fedorenko, Piantadosi, and Gibson: the communication-first correction Fedorenko, Piantadosi, and Gibson (2024) synthesise evidence that language is primarily a system for communication and cultural transmission rather than the substrate of all complex thought.

Neuropsychological dissociations, brain imaging, and the limited overlap between the language network and domain-general reasoning systems challenge the idea that thought is simply inner language.

Their account is a necessary counterweight to any expansive Whorfianism. The present thesis survives this correction because it does not identify language with cognition. Interfaces shape use without constituting the machine. A notation can transform science even though scientists also think visually, spatially, emotionally, and mathematically. The communication-first view may even strengthen the social version of the argument: because language coordinates many minds, small representational defaults can influence institutions and cumulative knowledge even when individual thought remains multimodal.

Gennari and the turn toward mechanism and taskGennari’s 2025 review argues that language effects in higher-order cognition must be situated in task goals, context, and available mechanisms. Linguistic categories may be recruited when a task demands fine discrimination, memory, verbal rehearsal, or social interpretation and may disappear under other conditions. This approach replaces the search for a global verdict with an analysis of when language becomes functionally relevant. That is the proper model for testing SLEnglish. Source tags should not be expected to improve every form of reasoning. They may help in delayed summarisation, contested claims, and multi-hop provenance while adding useless friction to direct perception. Identity operators may help in software lineage and institutional succession but not in ordinary object recognition. A design succeeds locally or not at all.
John, Macedo-Rouet, and Gros: current counterevidence mattersA preregistered 2026 study of 1,200 participants across English, French, German, and Mandarin tested whether language-specific temporal metaphors altered strategies for solving arithmetic word problems. The results favoured semantic content over linguistic metaphor: ordinal versus cardinal interpretations were driven by the problem structure rather than by the preferred spatialisation of time in the participants’ languages. This finding is valuable precisely because it is negative for a broad relativity claim. It demonstrates why the field must prefer preregistration, multi-language sampling, and task analysis to memorable one-off effects. The present project treats such failures as design information. A linguistic marker will not change reasoning merely because a philosopher can describe a conceptual difference. The marker must be recruited by the task, learned sufficiently, and connected to a useful operation.
WALS and Grambank: a design library with epistemic limitsThe World Atlas of Language Structures and Grambank transform typological knowledge into searchable comparative infrastructure. WALS offers curated chapters on evidentiality, clusivity, number, classifiers, tense, possession, and many other dimensions. Grambank aggregates more than four hundred thousand data points across more than 2,400 language varieties and 195 grammatical features. Together they make visible both the breadth of human design and the severe unevenness of documentation. Databases tempt users to treat a feature as a binary property detached from discourse, history, and analysis. “Has evidentiality” can conceal radically different systems; “lacks a feature” may mean that the grammar is incomplete or that analysts disagree. For conceptual invention, databases are hypothesis generators, not substitute grammars. Every promising comparison should return to specialist descriptions and, whenever possible, speakers.
Nicholson and Dupré: biology after substance ontologyEverything Flows: Towards a Processual Philosophy of Biology (Nicholson and Dupré 2018) argues that organisms and biological systems are better understood as stabilised processes than as substances that merely undergo change. Chapters by DiFrisco and Pradeu address criteria of identity, persistence, and genidentity in biological processes. The volume gives philosophical depth to the intuition behind the cloud, flame, and organism examples: continuity may consist in organised activity, causal dependence, and regulated replacement rather than sameness of matter. The relevance extends beyond biology. Software services, institutions, markets, and AI systems can also persist through controlled transformation. But process language should not become another universal solvent. Some legal and moral practices intentionally track bodies, persons, signatures, ownership, or lineage. The proposal is not to replace object ontology with process ontology; it is to mark which mode of identity a claim invokes.
The Problem of the Many: why a cloud is philosophically seriousWeatherson’s contemporary presentation of the Problem of the Many begins with a cloud whose boundary admits many nearly equivalent collections of droplets. If each candidate is equally eligible, ordinary singular reference appears to select one object without a fact that clearly privileges it. The puzzle connects vagueness, composition, identity, and counting. For this book, the cloud is not a decorative metaphor. It exposes a general error: grammar offers crisp singular nouns where the world offers graded boundaries and scale-dependent partitions. A boundary-grade marker would not solve the metaphysics, but it could prevent downstream systems from silently converting operational boundaries into natural ones. This matters in ecology, medical diagnosis, social groups, datasets, and algorithmic categories.
List, Pettit, and Tuomela: groups as agents under explicit criteriaList and Pettit’s Group Agency (2011) develops conditions under which organised groups can satisfy standards of agency through decision procedures that generate coherent beliefs and preferences. Tuomela’s Social Ontology (2013) distinguishes acting in the I-mode from acting in the we-mode and examines collective commitment and group reasons. These works provide a vocabulary richer than either individualism or loose anthropomorphism. The central design lesson is architectural. A company does not become an agent merely because a sentence places it before a verb. Its agency depends on mechanisms for representation, decision, correction, memory, and authorised action. Conversely, reducing every organisational outcome to isolated individuals can miss structural commitments and emergent constraints. Collective markers should therefore encode the path from members to act, not merely label the size of the subject.
Infinity across mathematics, physics, and metaphysicsThe 2025 revision of the Stanford Encyclopedia entry on infinity, by Easwaran, Hájek, Mancosu, and Oppy, treats infinity across mathematics, cosmology, physics, and theology. This breadth is essential because the word infinite conflates several questions: completed mathematical totalities, indefinitely extendable procedures, unbounded magnitudes, singularities in theories, spatial extent, divisibility, and divine attributes. The present book adds outfinit as a deliberately engineered concept: a process is outfinit when no final operational boundary is specified, although every realised stage remains finite and resource-bounded. The proposal does not compete with mathematical infinity. It isolates a practical form of open-endedness relevant to computation, scientific exploration, institutional evolution, and cultural production. Its value must be judged by whether it prevents invalid movement between “not yet bounded,” “too large to exhaust,” and “actually infinite.”
Cappelen, Chalmers, and the conceptual-engineering turnCappelen’s Fixing Language (2018), the volume Conceptual Engineering and Conceptual Ethics edited by Burgess, Cappelen, and Plunkett (2020), and Chalmers’s account of conceptual engineering shift philosophical attention from analysing inherited concepts to assessing, designing, and implementing better ones. They distinguish diagnosis from construction: showing that a concept is vague, harmful, or theoretically defective does not yet produce a replacement people can use. This literature gives the book its constructive method. A new distinction requires a target function, semantic specification, implementation path, adoption strategy, and evaluation. It also raises an ethical question often missing from engineered languages: who gains authority when a category becomes mandatory? A provenance system can protect inquiry or become surveillance. A responsibility vector can reveal institutional causation or provide bureaucratic theatre. Conceptual engineering must include governance and the right to contest the ontology.
Maat and the history of philosophical languagesMaat’s Philosophical Languages in the Seventeenth Century (2004) reconstructs the projects of Dalgarno, Wilkins, and Leibniz in their intellectual contexts. These schemes sought a systematic language whose symbols reflected an ordered catalogue of reality. Their ingenuity is inseparable from their fragility: once the taxonomy is disputed, the vocabulary becomes a monument to the designer’s assumptions. SLEnglish therefore rejects the dream of a complete language of things. It proposes small, revisable operators around recurrent reasoning failures. Its units need not encode the essence of a whale, state, or emotion. They can encode relations such as source, lineage, scale, and reversibility while leaving the underlying ontology open. The system should have versions, deprecations, competing profiles, and explicit unresolved categories.
Large language models as a new experimental siteMahowald and colleagues (2024) distinguish formal linguistic competence from functional competence in large language models. A system can produce well-formed language while lacking reliable world models, grounded goals, causal understanding, or calibrated use of evidence. This distinction mirrors the central argument of the book: richer expression is not automatically better thought. At the same time, language models make conceptual engineering unusually testable. Researchers can insert operators into prompts, intermediate representations, or training data and measure whether models retain provenance, distinguish forks from copies, or assign responsibility more accurately. Models can also act as adaptive interfaces that request a marker only when ambiguity threatens a decision. The danger is institutional scale: an unexamined schema embedded in widely used AI can harden one contested ontology into infrastructure.
What this map leaves openNo existing work combines all four movements attempted here: global typological comparison, cautious cognitive interpretation, metaphysics of identity and collectivity, and experimentally governed conceptual design. That combination is the opportunity, but also the main source of risk. Each field has standards that the others can easily violate. Linguistic categories can be oversimplified, experiments can be overgeneralised, metaphysical distinctions can be presented as facts of nature, and designed concepts can become moralised without evidence. The proper form of synthesis is therefore not a grand unification. It is an interface among disciplines with explicit type checking. Every passage should permit the reader to ask: Is this a fact about a language, a replicated behavioural effect, a philosophical model, or an invention proposed for trial? The book’s originality depends less on having unprecedented intuitions than on keeping those modes distinct while making them cooperate.
Controlled Natural Languages and executable semanticsTobias Kuhn’s survey provides the most useful map of the CNL design space and its trade-offs among precision, expressiveness, naturalness, and simplicity (2014). Attempto Controlled English demonstrates deterministic interpretation, discourse representation, querying, and theorem-proving integration. Common Logic Controlled English shows how English-like forms can express first-order semantics and role-based event structures. PENG demonstrates compilation into Answer Set Programming. SLEnglish should be evaluated as a deliberately less natural but more circuit-oriented member of this lineage, not as if the problem had no history.
Semantic Web standards as baselines and interoperability targetsRDF, OWL, SHACL, SPARQL, and PROV-O collectively cover graph representation, ontology semantics, validation, querying, and provenance. Their maturity makes them the strongest engineering baseline. The SLEnglish hypothesis concerns source uniformity and execution locality, not representational impossibility. A serious implementation must export and import useful subsets, document semantic loss, and avoid proprietary reinvention of standard concepts.
Datalog, ASP, constraints, and temporal reasoningDatalog provides a clean basis for recursive rule evaluation; Answer Set Programming supports non-monotonic rules, defaults, and optimisation; SMT and CSP systems support arithmetic and finite constraints; event calculi and temporal logics support changing worlds. SLEnglish should not conceal these differences behind one vague “reasoning engine.” Its profiles are contracts to established semantic families.
Vector Symbolic Architectures and Hyperdimensional ComputingPlate’s Holographic Reduced Representations and Kanerva’s account of hyperdimensional computing established a family of distributed compositional representations. Later surveys by Kleyko and colleagues systematise architectures, operations, hardware, and applications. Their role here is deliberately bounded: they may provide approximate memory and retrieval over exact semantic graphs. Whether that hybrid yields a net advantage is an experimental question.

Reproducible workflows and research objects FAIR workflow research, computational notebooks, provenance standards, and RO-Crate show how data and computations can become portable research objects. They also reveal the remaining separation between executable computation and prose-level claims.

Executable science should be understood as an extension of this ecosystem, not a replacement for it.
